## Page 1


Stability and instability of torus-symmetric Einstein
spacetimes with square-integrable connection
Bruno Le Floch∗and Philippe G. LeFloch†
May 2026
À Nounette, à l’occasion de ses vingt années cérésiennes
Abstract
We study the global evolution problem for the Einstein equations under T2 symmetry
on T3, allowing vacuum, scalar-field, and compressible-fluid matter models, governed
by a general equation of state including isothermal and polytropic fluids. Under this
symmetry, we obtain the first non-perturbative, global existence and stability theory
with connection coefficients being merely square-integrable, which allows both impulsive
gravitational waves and shock waves. In areal gauge, we introduce new fluid and geometric
variables and reformulate the Einstein–Euler system as a first-order system of nonlinear
balance laws with constraints and an entropy structure. The resulting formulation exhibits
hyperbolicity, null forms, entropy currents, div-curl structure, maximum principles, and
spacetime estimates.
This leads to a notion of tame Einstein–Euler flow for which
the essential geometric and fluid variables are square-integrable (finite energy), and the
secondary variables are absolutely continuous (or, more generally, of bounded variation).
In this non-perturbative and weak regularity setting, the equations remain meaningful
even when the Weyl curvature concentrates into Dirac masses along timelike hypersurfaces,
and the Ricci curvature remains only integrable. Our main results are a global existence
theorem for areal foliations, a nonlinear stability theorem for well-prepared initial data,
and a nonlinear instability theorem for geometrically oscillatory data, the latter producing
measure corrections to the stress energy tensor. In the future-contracting regime, the areal
foliation reaches a geometric singularity where the volume of T3 spatial slices degenerates
to zero. The areal function reaches zero generically in the non-vacuum Gowdy-symmetric
and vacuum torus-symmetric cases. In the future-expanding regime, the areal foliation is
complete, with both the spatial volume and the areal function tending to infinity at the
future boundary.

gravitational wave; shock wave.

Introduction
1.1
Global evolution in the non-perturbative regime
Matter spacetimes with T2 symmetry.
Non-perturbative regime. We study a class of globally hyperbolic spacetimes satisfying the
(vacuum or non-vacuum) Einstein equations. The existing mathematical literature on global
solutions most often focuses on small-data results, namely results restricted to spacetimes
that are close to known backgrounds such as Minkowski or Kerr spacetimes. Our goal is to
encompass large-amplitude configurations and singular spacetimes. To this end, we consider
the class of torus symmetric spacetimes, that is, spacetimes endowed with two commuting
spacelike Killing fields.
The natural questions are to parametrize such spacetimes by their initial data sets, solve
the corresponding initial value problem and investigate the stability or instability of sequences
of spacetimes. Our results in this paper1 describe the dynamics of matter fields evolving under
their own gravity in a non-perturbative regime.
More precisely, our methodology applies to a broad class of matter fields including vacuum
solutions, scalar fields, stiff fluids, as well as isothermal, polytropic, and more general perfect
fluids. Under the assumption of T2 symmetry on a spatial three-torus T3, we establish a global
theory of evolution and stability.
Beyond the formation of impulsive waves and shock waves. Studying such a broad class of
spacetimes requires overcoming several major challenges, which we first outlined in [34]. These
challenges arise from both the Weyl part of the Riemann curvature (which is defined only in
the sense of distributions) and the Ricci part (which is determined by the matter content).
• We allow for the presence of impulsive gravitational waves —a phenomenon that is
suppressed in the spherically symmetric setting. These waves propagate at the speed
1 An overview first appeared in 2019 as ArXiv:1912.1298 and was later published in [34].
4


## Page 5


of light and may lead to concentration phenomena throughout the spacetime. Our
framework encompasses such waves, for which the Weyl curvature may be defined only
in the sense of distributions.
• In addition, we allow for the presence of shock waves —a phenomenon that arises
generically in compressible fluid flows, even when the initial data are smooth. This is
a classical consequence of the nonlinear nature of the Euler equations, which makes
shock formation unavoidable in general. Accordingly, we must seek a global Cauchy
development that remains meaningful beyond the onset of shocks, while the Euler
equations (derived from the contracted second Bianchi identity satisfied by the geometry)
must be understood in a weak sense.
• Furthermore, the presence of fluid vacuum regions cannot in general be excluded, and
the stability theory must therefore encompass the transition between vacuum and non-
vacuum regions of spacetime. This is a delicate issue, since the mathematical techniques
required in these two regimes are quite different, and the Euler equations may fail to be
strictly hyperbolic at vacuum.
The mathematical analysis of spacetimes containing both gravitational waves and shock waves
requires dealing with weak solutions, as first recognized by Rendall and Stähl [60] (on shock
formation in plane symmetry) and LeFloch and Rendall [43] (on global foliations of Gowdy-
symmetric Einstein–Euler spacetimes); see also the lectures [41]. In the present paper, relying
on the strategy that we presented earlier by the authors in [34], we propose a framework
for defining Einstein–Euler spacetimes with weak regularity, and investigating their global
geometry and their stability properties under weak convergence.
Non-vacuum spacetimes with symmetry.
Scalar fields in the non-perturbative regime. We briefly review the works that have motivated
us to study Einstein–Euler spacetimes with symmetry in the large-data regime. (Section 1.4,
below, includes further related references).
As far as the global evolution problem is concerned, all currently available mathematical
results in the non-perturbative regime rely on some symmetry assumption. The first major
breakthrough in this direction was achieved by Christodoulou in his pioneering work on
self-gravitating scalar fields in spherical symmetry [8, 9]. In his early studies of the Einstein–
scalar field system in Bondi coordinates, he introduced a notion of generalized solution that
is C2 regular except possibly on the axis of symmetry. He subsequently developed a much
broader weak-regularity framework in which the metric coefficients have bounded variation.
This constituted one of the first systematic investigations of spacetimes with weak regularity,
including the analysis of the future boundary of global Cauchy developments, and it culminated
in his proof of Penrose’s weak cosmic censorship conjecture for scalar fields in spherical
symmetry for generic data. Christodoulou’s pioneering ideas have had a lasting influence and
have inspired a large body of subsequent research.
Kinetic matter in the non-perturbative regime. The study of spherically symmetric spacetimes
with kinetic matter governed by the Vlasov equation was initiated by Rendall [57], Andreas-
5


## Page 6


son [2], Andreasson and Rein [3], and followers. While the existence of global spacetime
foliations can be established by arguments similar to those developed for vacuum spacetimes,
it is significantly more challenging to understand their global geometry, since these spacetimes
may exhibit properties very different from those of their vacuum counterparts. In particular,
the coupling between the geometry and the kinetic distribution function introduces additional
analytical and structural difficulties. As a result, even in spherical symmetry, the kinetic-matter
case with large data remains considerably less developed than the corresponding vacuum and
fluid settings.
Compressible fluids in the non-perturbative regime. Despite the importance1 of the evolution
of self-gravitating compressible perfect fluids, the existing literature contains mainly formal
constructions of special classes of solutions, such as static fluids, together with formal asymptotic
analyses. No rigorous mathematical treatment of the non-perturbative problem was available
until recent years. Even under Gowdy symmetry, that is, when two-planes orthogonal to
symmetry orbits are integrable, only partial results have been obtained so far; cf. the pioneering
work by LeFloch and Rendall [43].
In particular, the results established in the present paper are new even in the Gowdy-
symmetric setting. Our methodology is to work within a class of weak solutions for which the
Einstein equations remain meaningful, admit large-data solutions, and can be analyzed by
weak convergence techniques such as compensated compactness (see below). This provides a
first step toward a rigorous analysis of the global geometry of such spacetimes and suggests a
methodology of interest well beyond the specific framework studied here.
On the other hand, on the Euler equations without symmetry restriction, major contri-
butions due to Christodoulou [12] and Speck et al. [1, 18, 51, 52, 66] are concerned with the
formation of shocks, whereas our focus in the present paper is on the evolution beyond shock
formation (and without specifically tracking the shock structure).
1.2
A new formulation of the Einstein–Euler system
Einstein–Euler system. By definition, a spacetime is a (3 + 1)-dimensional Lorentzian
manifold (M, g(1+3)) satisfying the Einstein equations2
G = T,
(1.1)
which relate the Einstein tensor G := Ric −(R/2)g(1+3), representing the geometry of spacetime,
to its matter content encoded by the stress-energy tensor T. By convention, the Lorentzian
signature is (−, +, +, +); here Ric denotes the Ricci curvature tensor associated with the
spacetime metric g(1+3), and R denotes the scalar curvature. Whenever necessary, we introduce
indices, with Greek indices ranging over {0, . . . , 3}. Indices are raised and lowered using the
metric, and we adopt the Einstein summation convention over repeated indices.
A perfect compressible fluid is governed by the stress-energy tensor
T = (µ + p(µ)) g(1+3)(u, ·) ⊗g(1+3)(u, ·) + p(µ) g(1+3),
(1.2)
1 For instance, in astrophysical or cosmological contexts.
2 We normalize the standard constant to unity.
6


## Page 7


where µ ⩾0 denotes the mass-energy density of the fluid, and u its future-oriented timelike
velocity field, normalized so that g(1+3)(u, u) = −1. By the second contracted Bianchi identities
for g(1+3), the Einstein equations imply the Euler equations:
div(1+3) T = 0,
(1.3)
where div(1+3) denotes the divergence operator associated with the Levi-Civita connection
of the spacetime metric g(1+3). We consider perfect fluids with stress-energy tensor (1.2),
governed by an equation of state p = p(µ) satisfying hyperbolicity conditions (stated in (1.8),
below).
Global Cauchy developments. When the initial data are sufficiently regular and a suitable
gauge is chosen (for instance the areal gauge in our T2-symmetry setup), the Einstein–Euler
equations form a locally well-posed evolution system of partial differential equations (of
hyperbolic type) with constraints. For any such initial data set, the Cauchy problem admits a
maximal globally hyperbolic Cauchy development [7, 73]. An initial data set for the Einstein–
Euler system (1.1)–(1.3) consists of a Riemannian manifold (M0, g0) together with a symmetric
two-tensor k0 defined on M0, as well as a mass-energy field µ0 ⩾0 and a vector field v0
defined on M0, satisfying suitable constraints, referred to as the Hamiltonian and momentum
constraints [7]. Solving the initial value problem for the Einstein equations from a given initial
data set consists of finding a Lorentzian manifold (M, g(1+3)) together with a scalar field
µ: M →[0, +∞) and a vector field u defined on M, such that the Einstein equations, and
hence the Euler equations, are satisfied. The future boundary of the Cauchy development
may exhibit singular behavior in the geometry, including impulsive gravitational waves, while
compressible fluids may develop singularities in finite time, namely shock waves. The purpose
of the present work is to formulate a weak regularity framework in which the Einstein–Euler
evolution remains meaningful beyond both types of singular behavior.
Proposed formulation. The central theme throughout the present study is the interplay
between the properties of the spacetime metric and those of the fluid unknowns. We proceed in
several steps, each step providing certain intermediate results of independent interest. Our first
objective is to exhibit certain hidden structural properties of the field equations. Remarkably,
as we show in Section 2, an analogue of the formulation we found in [33] for vacuum Gowdy
spacetimes is available for the general class of T2 symmetric matter spacetimes, even when the
fluid is governed by a general equation of state. A difficulty we overcome is that the so-called
twist variables need not be constants, in contrast with the ‘Gowdy matter’ or ‘T2 vacuum’ cases.
Interestingly, our choice of variables and exponents is rigidly fixed by requiring cubic terms not
to appear. In particular, for the description of the fluid we introduce suitably metric-weighted
variables, needed to allow for the mass-energy density to vanish in some spacetime region
—which is necessary since vacuum regions can arise from non-vacuum initial data.
In the gauge under consideration, the field equations (1.1)–(1.3) take the form of a system
of partial differential equations and include nonlinear hyperbolic equations whose solutions
generically develop singularities from initially regular data. The field equations couple metric
and matter unknowns together, which makes the whole analysis particularly challenging. More
7


## Page 8


precisely, in areal gauge the spacetime metric g(1+3) depends upon six metric coefficients and
satisfies a system of nonlinear wave equations coupled to differential equations. The solutions
typically become singular at some final time in the contracting direction when the area function
approaches zero. On the other hand, the matter variables, i.e. the mass-energy density µ ⩾0
and the velocity vector u satisfy a nonlinear hyperbolic system of two coupled balance laws.
The main challenge to be overcome is to elucidate the nature of the coupling between the
geometric equations of wave map type and nonlinear hyperbolic equations. The wave map
structure has attracted a lot of attention in recent years; see [31, 67, 70] and the references
therein, as well as Kenig [29] for a study of the blow-up mechanism in focusing wave equations.
1.3
Main results
Areal flows with weak regularity.
Geometric setup. The T2-symmetric spacetimes under consideration admit a foliation by
compact spacelike hypersurfaces Mt, each diffeomorphic to T3, that is,
M =

Mt,
(1.4)
where the time function t : M →I ⊂R parametrizes the leaves of the foliation and agrees, up
to sign, with the area of the T2-orbits of symmetry. This choice of time variable is called the
areal gauge. An initial data set, denoted by (˚g,˚k,˚µ,˚v), consists of T2-invariant data on T3 for
which all symmetry orbits have the same area. A corresponding spacetime solution consists of
a T2-invariant Lorentzian metric g(1+3), a scalar field µ ⩾0, and a unit timelike vector field u.
The quantity ˚µ˚v is interpreted as the projection onto the initial hypersurface of the matter
momentum µu, and is tangent to that hypersurface.
The density ˚µ is allowed to vanish; in that case, the velocity field u itself is no longer
intrinsically defined, and similarly for ˚v. This vacuum degeneracy is a major technical difficulty
in the analysis of the Euler equations. We may describe the solution to the Einstein equation
as a T2 symmetric flow on the torus T3 consisting of
• a Riemannian 3-metric g(t) and a two-tensor k(t) —representing the second fundamental
form of each spatial slice, and
• a scalar field µ(t) ⩾0, and a vector field v(t) —representing the projection of the physical
velocity field.
Weak regularity setup. The weak regularity conditions will be introduced in the course of our
analysis: roughly speaking, k(t) and the first-order space derivatives of g(t) must be square-
integrable on spacelike slices, while the mass-energy density µ(t) is integrable and velocity
components parallel to the T2 orbits of symmetry obey suitable uniform bounds. Consequently,
the Weyl curvature includes derivatives of square-integrable functions, which are defined only
in the sense of distributions, while the Ricci curvature is integrable. While in the present paper
we will state the weak regularity conditions in areal gauge, our weak regularity conditions can
be also expressed in a different gauge by working out the regularity of the fields [36].
8


## Page 9


Global future evolution. The remarkable structure of the Einstein equations (in T2-
symmetry) exhibited in Section 2, below, plays a central role in order to investigate the
nonlinear geometry-fluid coupling and tackle the global evolution problem for the Einstein
equations.
Our main statements, presented in this introduction in a preliminary form, apply to
compressible perfect fluids governed by a general pressure law (satisfying hyperbolicity and
convexity conditions (1.8)), which we assume to be asymptotically polytropic or isothermal
in the vicinity of the vacuum state, and to be asymptotic isothermal for large values of the
mass-energy density. In Definition 1.3, below, we define this class referred to as an admissible
equation of state.
Moreover, in the presence of shock waves, the Euler equations are no longer time-reversible.
It is therefore physically meaningless to evolve fluid solutions backward across shocks. This
naturally leads us to distinguish between two classes of initial data sets, corresponding
respectively to future-expanding and future-contracting spacetimes.
As in the standard
theory of compressible fluids, this irreversibility is encoded by an entropy inequality. This
feature plays a fundamental role in the present theory and is addressed in Section 3, through the
introduction of entropy currents, and in Section 4, through the definition of weak solutions and
entropy inequalities. For clarity of exposition, we refer to our class of weak solutions as tame
solutions; cf. Definition 4.2 for the notion of Einstein–Euler flow, together with Definition 4.4
for the definition of a tame Einstein–Euler flow.
By definition, the tame regularity assumptions impose Sobolev H1-type bounds on the
principal metric coefficients (therefore L2 integrability at the level of the connection coefficients),
suitable Lebesgue integrability conditions on the fluid variables, and absolute continuity together
with finite variation bounds on the auxiliary metric coefficients and on a rescaled version of
the momentum component parallel to the symmetry orbits.
Theorem 1.1 (Global foliation of T2-symmetric spacetimes with weak regularity). Consider
the initial value problem associated with the Einstein–Euler system (1.1)–(1.3), describing the
global evolution of a compressible perfect fluid governed by an admissible equation of state (in
the sense of Definition 1.3 below). Suppose that the initial data set (˚g,˚k,˚µ,˚v) is T2-symmetric
on the three-torus T3with orbits of constant area |t0|, where the sign of t0 ̸= 0 determines
whether the data are of future-contracting or future-expanding type. Assume moreover that the
data have tame regularity (cf. Section 4) and satisfies Einstein’s constraint equations in the
weak sense.
Then this initial data set generates a future Cauchy development which is a tame solution1
(M, g(1+3), µ, u) of the Einstein–Euler equations, understood in the sense of distributions2.
This solution also satisfies suitable entropy inequalities and enjoys the following global geometric
properties. It may be viewed as a T2-flow on T3, endowed with a foliation by a time function
t: M →I ⊂R, whose leaves all have topology T3 and are T2-symmetric, with symmetry orbits
of area |t|.
1 In the same regularity class as the initial data set.
2 In particular, the Ricci tensor is Lebesgue integrable, while the Weyl tensor is at most a first-order
distribution.
9


## Page 10


• Future-expanding regime (t0 > 0): one has M = [t0, +∞) × T3, and the areal
foliation is complete toward the future, in the sense that both the volume of the T3-slices
and the area of the T2-orbits tend to infinity.
• Future-contracting regime (t0 < 0): one has M = [t0, t∗) × T3, where the areal
foliation extends up to some time t∗∈(t0, 0] such that the volume of the T3-slices tends to
zero as t →t∗. Moreover, either t∗< 0, in which case the conformal length of the quotient
manifold T3/T2 tends to zero, or t∗= 0, in which case the area of the T2-orbits tends
to zero. For vacuum T2-symmetric spacetimes, or for non-vacuum Gowdy-symmetric
spacetimes, the latter alternative t∗= 0 holds generically1.
Nonlinear stability and instability. Our second main result concerns sequences of space-
times. The statement below is relevant (and new) even for vacuum spacetimes and generalizes
our earlier study of (vacuum) Gowdy spacetimes [33], while supplementing [43] (for the Euler
system with Gowdy symmetry). The notions of flow with finite energy and corrector stress
tensor will be introduced later in Section 4, and a detailed statement will be provided in
Theorem 5.9, below.
Theorem 1.2 (Nonlinear stability and instability of T2 symmetric spacetimes). Under the
conditions in Theorem 1.1, consider a sequence of T2 symmetric, either future-expanding or
future-contracting initial data sets (gϵ

The upper bound p′(µ) < 1 rules out the possibility that the sound speed approach the speed
of light. The condition 0 < p′(µ) for µ > 0 implies that the Euler equations form a strictly
hyperbolic system away from vacuum, while the assumptions p(0) = 0 and p′(0) < 1 ensure
that p(µ) < µ for µ > 0. Meanwhile, the lower bound in (1.8) is a mild restriction ensuring
that the pressure is not too small, thereby preventing the fluid from concentrating at arbitrarily
large density values. Furthermore, the condition (1.8b) implies that the characteristic fields of
the relativistic Euler equations are genuinely nonlinear (in the sense of Lax).
For instance, our results apply to isothermal fluids with p = k2µ, which clearly satisfy these
conditions, provided the speed of sound k ∈(0, 1) remains below the speed of light (normalized
to unity). They also apply to equations of states that asymptote to a polytropic or isothermal
law p = µγ with exponent γ ⩾1 at low densities µ →0 and to the isothermal case at large
densities. More precisely, in addition to (1.8), mild asymptotic conditions at µ →0, +∞are

1.4
Further background and related works
Sufficiently regular and vacuum spacetimes with T2 symmetry on T3. Over the past
thirty years, significant progress has been made on the initial value problem for the Einstein
equations in the non-perturbative regime, provided the gravitational and matter fields are
assumed to be sufficiently regular. In addition to the references already cited in Section 1.1,
we would like to emphasize the following results for the class of spacetimes with T2 symmetry
on T3. Moncrief [54, 19] first provided a full treatment of the Cauchy problem for in the
polarized Gowdy case. A series of works followed (for which we refer to Rendall’s textbook [58]),
establishing the existence of a global foliation by spacelike hypersurfaces in the areal gauge.
After work by Berger et al. [5], the existence problem was finally closed by Isenberg and
Weaver [27]: they established that the corresponding areal time function covers the entire
range (0, +∞), describing an evolution originating from a Big Bang and dispersing indefinitely
toward the future.
We do not attempt here to review the vast literature on this class of spacetimes. Beyond the
issue of the existence of global foliations, and even for vacuum spacetimes, many challenging
questions remain open concerning the global geometric behavior of T2-symmetric vacuum
spacetimes, including geodesic completeness, curvature blow-up, and the Penrose conjecture.
It is only for the restricted class of Gowdy spacetimes that the behavior near the cosmological
singularity is now well understood, thanks to pioneering work by Ringström [61, 62, 63]. In the
expanding direction, we refer to [44] for partial results on the geometry of the future boundary.
Metrics with weak regularity.
1 Strictly speaking, in our companion paper [36] devoted to relativistic fluids, we also require a very mild
integrability condition on the deviation p(µ) −cγµγ near vacuum, and likewise near infinity.
12


## Page 13


General metrics. A definition of the Einstein equations for general metrics (without symmetry
restriction) in the sense of distributions was proposed by several authors, including [42, 49]
(and the references therein). However, no existence result is available in such a setup, and only
junction conditions can be derived; cf. for instance [28, 15, 53, 35].
Bounded variation. As already pointed out in this introduction, earlier on, Christodoulou
defined the class of spherically symmetric spacetimes with bounded variation [8, 9], and proved
that singularities in spherically symmetric spacetimes arise at the center of symmetry; see also
[45]. In the bounded variation class, the coupling with the Euler equations was studied first in
spherical symmetry (and in Bondi coordinates) in [22, 6].
In contrast, gravitational singularities in T2 symmetry propagate and require an evolution
system of their own: in Gowdy symmetry and areal coordinates by [4], solutions with bounded
variation were investigated in [23].
Sobolev regularity. Solutions in a suitable Sobolev regularity class were considered more recently.
The first work on solutions with shock waves and impulsive waves to the Einstein–Euler system
was LeFloch and Rendall [43] who constructed a global areal foliation. Using Penrose’s spinor
formalism, LeFloch and Stewart [46, 47] analyzed the characteristic initial value problem for
plane-symmetric spacetimes with weak regularity. Next, weakly regular vacuum spacetimes
with T2 symmetry were defined and studied in [44]; in addition, the class of Gowdy spacetimes
was particularly investigated in [33].
Dynamics of compressible fluids with shocks. Importantly, our work deals with weak
solutions that may have arbitrary large amplitude and may contain vacuum regions, by
extending known techniques to the Einstein–Euler spacetimes under consideration. This
requires new observations concerning the nonlinear coupling between the fluid and the geometric
parts for the Cauchy problem. In particular, we must consider balance laws and cope with
geometric corrections to the fluid dynamics, which are of lower order in the fluid variables and
thus turn out to be controlled at the level of weak regularity under consideration.
The mathematical analysis of compressible fluid flows was initiated by DiPerna in his
breakthrough work [16, 17]. While bounded solutions were treated by DiPerna, the generaliza-
tion to the broader class of solutions enjoying natural energy bounds (as we do in the present
paper) was discovered only more recently by LeFloch and Westdickenberg [48] and extended
by Germain and LeFloch [20].
Let us recall that several challenges arise with fluid flows containing shock waves. Due
to the generic phenomenon of shock formation from smooth initial data, only weak solutions
understood in the distributional sense can be sought for. Moreover, vacuum regions with
µ = 0 (or J0 = 0) may appear in finite time from non-vacuum initial data; in such a vacuum
region, the Euler equations are trivially satisfied since all flux terms are proportional to J0.
This property is consistent with the physical intuition that the velocity is ill-defined in the
absence of a fluid, but must also be taken into account in designing a notion of solution. In this
regime, the equations (stated in (2.30c)–(2.30d), below) satisfied by the parallel momentum are
trivially satisfied while the equations for the orthogonal momentum reduce to energy equations
for the metric coefficients only. This difficulty is unavoidable when dealing with compressible
13


## Page 14


matter, since vacuum regions may arise from non-vacuum initial data.
Back-reaction phenomenon and oscillatory limits. The instability case of Theorem 1.2
above may be viewed as a realization of Einstein’s intuition that matter terms1 could arise
as “singularities” of the gravitational field. In the physics literature, matter terms arising
from oscillatory limits of smooth vacuum spacetimes are discussed under the terminology of
back-reaction from high-frequency waves. In particular, small-scale inhomogeneities and their
effective contribution to the large-scale dynamics have been investigated in cosmology by Green
and Wald [21] and the references therein.
Rigorous mathematical statements were established by the authors [33] for the class of
Gowdy spacetimes and, without symmetry assumptions, in the work of Lott [50], Huneau
and Luk [24, 25, 26], and Touati [71, 72], by developping argument of (trilinear) compensated
compactness for sufficiently regular spacetimes —without symmetry restriction. See also the
historical review by Kiessling and Tahvildar-Zadeh [30].
Our result fits within this broad perspective and, on the present symmetry class, is the
first mathematical result in a weak regularity setting, proving that oscillations in the geometry
generate effective matter terms in the limit, which we encode by a corrector stress-energy tensor.
We also mention that other interesting asymptotic regimes for matter models have recently
been studied, for instance by Petropoulos et al. in connection with the so-called Carrollian
limit [56].
1.5
Overview of Part I: the theory of T2-symmetric Einstein spacetimes
with weak regularity
The JKL first-order formulation (Section 2). The formulation introduced in this paper is
presented in Definition 2.2. Its main unknowns are the fields J⊥, J∥, K, and L on the quotient
manifold N = M/T2, together with the quotient metric gN described by the lapse function Ω
and the conformal length density λ associated with an areal foliation; cf. Section 2.1. More
precisely:
• J⊥is a real-valued one-form describing the component of the fluid velocity orthogonal
to the T2 orbits, pushed forward to N and suitably weighted by the fluid density and
pressure.
• J∥is a complex-valued scalar field encoding the weighted fluid velocity parallel to the
T2 orbits.
• K is a complex-valued scalar field describing the two twist parameters.
• L = i dτ/ Im τ is a complex-valued one-form encoding the modular parameter τ ∈C,
Im τ > 0, of the T2 orbits.
1 The authors first became aware of this phenomenon through discussions with Luc Tartar, who understood
it as arising naturally from the theory of compensated compactness and as expressing, at the physical level, the
emergence of effective matter from oscillatory gravitational degrees of freedom.
14


## Page 15


Complex-valued fields arise naturally when parametrizing the T2 orbits as discrete quotients
of C. With the notation
J = (J⊥, J∥),
L = P + i Q,
(1.10)
our first-order system of evolution and constraint equations for (J, K, L) consists of
• four nonlinear hyperbolic balance laws for J,
• four evolution and constraint equations for K,
• four nonlinear wave equations for L,
• together with two evolution equations for the conformal length density λ and lapse
function Ω, and one constraint equation for Ω.
These equations are stated in (2.29)–(2.32), below, and their geometric and analytic structure
is described in Proposition 2.4. This choice of variables is especially well suited to the weak
regularity theory developed in this paper, and already reveals several key features of the
Einstein–Euler system, including the entropy current and quasi-current structures discussed in
Section 3.
Hidden entropy structure of the Euler equations (Section 3). The next section uncovers
one important feature: the Euler equations in torus symmetry possess a rich hidden entropy
structure. This structure is the basis of our weak solution framework and of the compactness
arguments used later in the global existence and stability theory. Starting from the geometric
notion of entropy current introduced in Definition 3.1, we present several nontrivial families of
balance laws for the Euler system. These include the particle number current (3.24), whose
main properties are summarized in (3.25)–(3.26), as well as entropy currents associated with
the parallel momentum variables through (3.30) and (3.35).
A key point is that the standard notion of entropy current is not sufficient for the compact-
ness analysis, except in situations where the parallel fluid components are assumed to vanish.
This leads us to introduce a broader class of objects, called quasi-currents; see Definition 3.7.
In curved geometry these satisfy quasi-balance laws (derived in (3.50), below) and, together
with a convexity notion (cf. Proposition 3.11) they provide the flexible entropy framework
needed for compensated compactness and for the continuation of the Einstein–Euler evolution
beyond shock formation.
The class of tame Einstein–Euler flows (Section 4). The aim is to formulate the
Einstein–Euler system at the weak regularity level naturally associated with the evolution
and constraint equations. First of all, the notion of ΦΨ flow with finite energy, given in
Definition 4.1, distinguishes between the L2-type variables
Φ = (J⊥, J∥, P, Q)
(1.11)
and the BV -type variables
Ψ = (ℓ, log Ω, K),
(1.12)
15


## Page 16


which, by definition, satisfy integrability and weak regularity conditions (cf. (4.13)) relative to
the natural spacelike and timelike volume forms, denoted by dvol(3) and dvol(1+2) (cf. (4.3)).
These assumptions are dictated by the nonlinear structure of the first-order system. At first,
all equations except the wave equation for the lapse are required to hold in the sense of
distributions through a weak formulation of the Einstein equations and the fluid equations
equations; cf. Definition 4.2. We show the Einstein constraint equations propagate throughout
the whole time interval, as stated in Proposition 4.5.
Next, building on the entropy structure derived in Section 3, we introduce our central
notion, namely the class of tame solutions. Specifically, we strengthen the weak formulation by
requiring certain entropy balance laws to hold in the sense of distributions; see Definition 4.4.
This framework includes quasi-balance inequalities (weak analogues of (3.50)) associated with
quasi-currents dominated by the reference current M 0•.
Thus, the hidden entropy structure uncovered in Section 3 becomes an essential ingredient
in the definition of a robust class of weak solutions, one that will be shown to be stable under
weak convergence. In this sense, Section 4 forms the backbone of the present work: it introduces
a notion of solution weak enough to ensure existence for the Cauchy problem, yet strong enough
to provide the compactness properties needed for the analysis of the Einstein–Euler system.
Compactness, stability, and instability (Section 5). A central objective of the paper is
to analyze the nonlinear stability properties of Einstein flows in T2 symmetry. To this end,
in Section 5, we study suitably bounded sequences of tame Einstein–Euler flows, namely by
assuming uniform bounds on the natural L2 and BV norms, and we determine their possible
weak limits. This yields a compactness framework broad enough to encompass perturbations
of initial data, vanishing-viscosity approximations, and finite volume schemes.
The analysis relies on the decomposition into L2-type variables Φ = (J⊥, J∥, P, Q) and
BV -type variables Ψ = (ℓ, log Ω, K), together with uniform bounds in the natural norms. It is
sufficient to assume that the field equations hold (possibly) only approximately, up to negligible
H−1 and measure error terms, and the entropy inequalities are imposed in generalized form
as well. This leads to a comprehensive notion of tame sequence of Einstein–Euler flows, as
introduced in Definition 5.2.
The section begins by stating a general compactness result for sequences of flows with
corrector. More precisely, Theorem 5.3 shows that every tame sequence of (approximate)
Einstein–Euler flows with corrector admits, after extraction of a subsequence, a limiting tame
Einstein–Euler flow with corrector. This yields the nonlinear instability mechanism in its most
general form, since generally speaking the sequence of corrector measures does not converge to
the corrector measure of the limiting flow.
The notion of initially well-prepared sequence is then introduced in Definition 5.4, and
the general instability theorem is refined into a stability statement for the corrector itself; see
Corollary 5.5. By specializing to sequences without corrector, we obtain the nonlinear stability
theorem for Einstein–Euler flows stated in Theorem 5.6. Finally, Corollary 5.8 extends these
compactness results to include (approximate) initial data sets.
Hence, Section 5 identifies both the stability mechanism and the instability mechanism
governing weak limits of Einstein–Euler flows in the non-perturbative regime.

1.6
Overview of Part II: the finite energy method for T2-symmetric Einstein
spacetimes
A priori estimates for Einstein–Euler flows (Section 6). The second part of this paper
is devoted to the proof of the main results. An important task is the derivation of a priori
estimates for weak solutions, which provide the analytic backbone of the whole weak regularity
and compactness theory. We begin by introducing a geometric energy of an Einstein–matter
spacetime (M, g(1+3), µ, u) with T2 symmetry on T3, namely the function E of the areal time t
defined by
E(t) := 2
|t|

T3 Le0
 dvol(3)
.
(1.13)
Here, g(3) denotes the Riemannian metric induced on the slices of constant areal time t, dvol(3)
is its volume form, e0 is the future-pointing timelike unit vector field orthogonal to these
slices1, and L denotes the Lie derivative. Thus, E(t) measures how the volume of a slice of
constant areal time varies under shifts by constant proper time. Once the Einstein equations
are imposed, this functional becomes a natural square-integrability norm for the first-order
geometric variables and the matter fields.
In Section 6, starting from an arbitrary tame Einstein–Euler flow, possibly with corrector,
and prescribed initial data, we derive a collection of properties: monotonicity, integrability, and
pointwise boundedness on both the geometric and fluid variables. A first group of estimates
concerns the spacelike volume V(t), the conformal length functional Lconf(t), and the energy E(t)
itself (defined in (6.1), (6.9), and (6.16), below). The corresponding estimates are stated in
Lemmas 6.1, 6.2, and 6.5, below. These quantities enjoy monotonicity properties along the
areal foliation, yielding a quantitative control of the geometry in both the expanding and
contracting regimes.
A second group of estimates provides two-sided bounds for the lapse Ω, control of the
conformal length measure dxℓ, and pointwise estimates for the twist variables K, which are
stated in Lemmas 6.7, 6.9, and 6.8, below. We also derive spacetime and timelike-slice estimates
for the main first-order variables. Altogether, these estimates show that the weak regularity
assumptions introduced in Section 4 are propagated by the Einstein–Euler evolution and provide
the uniform bounds needed later in the nonlinear stability and instability theory.
A priori estimates for tame Einstein–Euler flows (Section 7). The next section
complements the a priori estimates of Section 6 by deriving maximum principles and additional
integrability properties for the fluid and geometric variables. A first objective is to refine our
control of the parallel momentum and the twist variables in sup norm. For sufficiently regular
solutions, this can be seen directly from the transport equations satisfied by the metric-weighted
quantities eJ∥and eK, which are propagated along the fluid characteristics. This leads to the
maximum principle stated in Lemma 7.3, showing that the metric-weighted parallel momentum
and twist functions remain bounded pointwise in terms of their initial values. A second, more
robust, argument is then developed at the level of weak solutions. It relies on the entropy
1 We do not need to distinguish explicitly the frame vectors e0, e1 on N and the corresponding frame vectors
on M orthogonal to the T2 symmetry orbits, since they are identified by push-forward along the quotient map.
17


## Page 18


structure introduced in Section 3, and more precisely on the H-divergence law (as we call it)
satisfied by currents of the form H( eJ∥)N and H( eK)N; cf. (7.5). This entropy-based method
yields the same maximum principles without appealing to characteristics, and therefore applies
in the weak regularity setting relevant to the global theory.
The section then turns to an additional integrability property for the Einstein–Euler system.
By combining the Euler equations with the estimates established earlier for the lapse, the
twists, and the energy, we derive a nonlinear integral estimate involving suitable quadratic
and quartic expressions built from P, Q, J⊥, and J∥(see Lemma 7.6, below). In particular,
we obtain spacetime control of null forms such as X · Y and X ∧Y, of |J · J|, and of the
parallel momentum in L4. Together with the maximum principles, these estimates provide the
integrability properties needed to justify the entropy inequalities, to control nonlinear products,
and ultimately to carry out the compensated compactness argument.
Compactness properties of metric components (Section 8). Next, we establish the
compactness properties needed in the stability and instability theorems of Section 5. Our
strategy is hierarchical. Starting from a bounded sequence of tame flows, we first analyze the
geometric variables: the conformal length and the lapse, then the essential geometry variables
P, Q, and finally the twist functions. These variables satisfy either linear transport equations
or div-curl systems whose source terms are controlled by the estimates derived in Section 6
and Section 7. In the well-prepared regime, this yields strong compactness of the geometry; for
general data, it yields a weaker compactness statement, still sufficient to pass to the limit, but
allowing the appearance of a corrector in the lapse equations.
A central motivation for the compactness analysis is the following general mechanism,
which goes back to compensated compactness in the sense of Tartar [68, 69] and Murat [55]. In
our formulation, Einstein’s equations couple nonlinear hyperbolic balance laws with generalized
wave-map equations, and the nonlinear terms are organized so as to exhibit a div-curl structure.
The relevant phenomenon is already visible in the following model, in arbitrary spatial dimension
N ⩾1:

is relatively compact in H−1, then any weak limit ϕ♯= (ϕ♯

Indeed, the first derivatives of ϕϵ have the structure of gradients, hence satisfy the corre-
sponding curl-free compatibility relations, while the wave equations control their divergences.
The div-curl lemma therefore applies, and the quadratic null forms are stable under weak
convergence. In the framework of the present paper, we further establish spacetime integrability
properties ensuring that the relevant quadratic expressions are bounded in L1; by Murat’s
lemma, this is sufficient to obtain the required H−1 compactness. This is the fundamental
compactness mechanism underlying the whole paper. It hints at how the entropy structure, the
maximum principles, the additional integrability estimates, and the div-curl structure interact
so as to allow passage to the limit in the full Einstein–Euler system.
Compactness properties of fluid variables (Section 9). The fluid variables require a
separate argument. The general methodology for dealing with weak solutions to the compressible
Euler equations goes back to the pioneering work of DiPerna [16, 17] and its subsequent
developments. In the present paper, we are especially motivated by the extension of DiPerna’s
theory to (possibly unbounded) weak solutions with finite energy, a class of solutions first defined
and investigated by LeFloch and Westdickenberg [48]. A major obstacle is the construction of
a sufficiently rich class of nonlinear balance laws and entropy inequalities, together with an
analysis of how geometric and matter terms are coupled within these laws. This is precisely
the purpose of both Sections 3 and 9, which are also supplemented with our follow-up paper
[36], which present a comprehensive theory of relativistic fluid flows with symmetry.
For the orthogonal momentum, we exploit the entropy quasi-currents introduced in Section 3.
Their divergences are shown to be H−1-compact, which allows us to apply a metric-weighted
div-curl lemma and derive a Tartar commutation relation for the associated Young measure.
Since the available class of quasi-currents is sufficiently large, this commutation relation forces
the Young measure to collapse to a Dirac mass, and therefore yields strong convergence of
the orthogonal fluid momentum. The parallel momentum is treated separately through the
transport structure and maximum principles established in Section 7, together with compactness
derived from a bounded variation bound.
In turn, in combination with the results in Section 8, we prove both the nonlinear stability
result for well-prepared data and the instability statement for general data, the latter through
the appearance of a stress-energy corrector in the limiting geometry.
Concluding observations (Section 10).
Another choice of reference entropy. The final section is devoted to several extensions and
complements of the theory developed in this paper. Its first purpose is to compare two natural
entropy frameworks for the Euler equations in the present geometric setting. In the formulation
adopted throughout the core of the paper, the particle number current N is kept as an exact
divergence equality, while the stress-energy current M0• is treated as the reference entropy
current and therefore satisfies an entropy inequality. In Section 10, we also discuss the reverse
choice, in which M0• is kept in exact balance form and the particle number current is taken
19


## Page 20


as the reference entropy current; cf. (10.1). Although both formulations agree at the level of
regular solutions, they lead to different theories of weak solutions once shocks are present.
The two entropy frameworks do not have the same scope. The formulation developed in
the present paper, based on the reference entropy current M0•, allows for nontrivial parallel
momentum, namely J2 ̸≡0 and J3 ̸≡0, and is therefore adapted to the full Einstein–Euler
system in torus symmetry. By contrast, the alternative formulation based on the particle
number current is restricted1 to the reduced regime J2 = J3 = 0, in which the quasi-currents
introduced earlier reduce to standard entropy currents. This comparison clarifies the structural
role of our entropy choice and explains why the framework based on M0• is the one best suited
to the non-perturbative regime considered in this paper.
Other matter models. Beyond compressible fluids, Section 10 indicates that the methods
developed here apply more broadly. Scalar fields fit naturally into the present first-order
framework, while a cosmological constant Λ introduces only lower-order modifications and
leaves the main hyperbolic, entropy, and compactness structures unchanged. More generally,
the discussion encompasses vacuum, scalar fields, and several fluid models, and points toward
further extensions to other symmetry classes.
More conceptually, under symmetry, the Einstein equations retain a robust nonlinear
structure even at weak regularity, provided one works with suitable first-order variables and an
adapted entropy framework. This structure is flexible enough to accommodate both fluid shocks
and impulsive gravitational waves, while still allowing one to formulate a global evolution and
compactness theory.
The paper also includes an appendix containing technical material: expressions of the
Vielbein and spin connection (Appendix A), simplified expressions for isothermal fluids (Ap-
pendix B), structure of the Einstein–Euler system (Appendix C), further properties of the
Euler equations (Appendix D), and a density property (Appendix E).
1.7
Summary of notation
For the reader’s convenience, we collect here the main notation used throughout the paper.

Part I
The theory of T2-symmetric Einstein
spacetimes with weak regularity
2
The JKL first-order formulation
2.1
Spacetime metrics in areal gauge
Global areal time. Throughout, we assume a T2 symmetric spacetime with T3 spatial topology,
and we foliate the spacetime (M, g(1+3)) under consideration with spacelike hypersurfaces of
constant areal time denoted by t. Namely, we introduce a global time coordinate t: M →
I ⊂R \ {0} that coincides (up to a sign) with the area of the T2-orbits of symmetry. Here, I
denotes a (compact or non-compact) interval that does not contain 0. Most of our definitions
and results will be stated on a compact interval I = [t0, t1]; however, our definition extends
immediately to semi-open intervals I = [t0, t∗) ⊂(0, +∞) or I = [t0, t∗) ⊂(−∞, 0). Einstein’s
constraint equations, along with the positive energy condition satisfied by the matter model,
imply that the gradient ∇|t| of the area function is a timelike vector field (cf. [58] and the
references cited therein). We define the sign of t so that ∇t is future-oriented. Hence, positive t
and negative t correspond to future-expanding and future-contracting spacetimes, respectively.
Furthermore, in this section, we assume that all objects under consideration are sufficiently
regular.
Modular parameter. Besides the area |t|, the metric induced on each T2 orbit is characterized
by the modular parameter, denoted τ ∈C with Im τ > 0, which describes the conformal
structure of the discrete quotient C/(Z + τZ). The modular parameter is defined up to discrete
transformations
τ 7→(aτ + b)/(cτ + d) with
 
a
b
c
d
!
∈SL(2, Z),
(2.1)
which can be gauge-fixed by choosing a pair of reference one-cycles inside T2 (with intersection
number 1). We focus our attention on spacetimes whose spacelike hypersurfaces have T3
topology, ensuring a global choice of such cycles; thus the modular parameter τ is periodic in
space. For the remainder of this paper, we decompose τ = Q + ie−P into its real and (positive)
imaginary parts. Although we assume T3 topology, our method is general and should also
apply to the case where Cauchy surfaces are realized as certain non-trivial T2 bundles over
the circle S1, including the comparatively simpler case of (vacuum) twisted Gowdy spacetimes
introduced in [59]. In that setting, the spatial periodicity of τ is replaced by periodicity up to
an SL(2, Z) transformation.
Lapse function and conformal length density. We consider the (two-dimensional) quo-
tient manifold N := M/T2 and the associated quotient metric gN , defined from the four-
dimensional metric via the assumed action by the T2 group of symmetries. By construction,
the areal time t remains constant along the T2 orbits, and thus serves as a time coordinate on
23


## Page 24


this (1 + 1)-dimensional Lorentzian manifold. We define the conformal factor Ω> 0 via the
squared norm g−1
N (dt, dt), namely
Ω−2 := −g−1
N (dt, dt).
(2.2)
In a spatial coordinate x chosen so that its level sets are orthogonal to those of t, the quotient
metric gN on N takes the form
gN = Ω2(−dt2 + λ2dx2).
(2.3)
More precisely, we define the coordinate x (up to an overall constant shift) along the initial
data surface {t = t0} ≃T3/T2 by prescribing1 the function λ|t=t0 such that x is periodic with
period 1. The results we establish in this paper ensure the existence of a coordinate x for
which the quotient metric takes the form (2.3).
The conformal coefficient Ωis commonly referred to as the lapse function of the foliation,
while we will dub the function λ the conformal length density. It is an (inverse) speed of
light relating the null slopes in the quotient metric gN = Ω2(−dt2 + λ2dx2) and therefore will
appear as a speed in key differential operators relevant for the geometry variables.
Adapted moving frame. We now construct an adapted moving frame, namely an orthonormal
basis of vector fields on M
e0, e1, e2, e3 that remain invariant under the T2 symmetry.
(2.4)
First, e0 is the future-directed unit vector normal to constant-t hypersurfaces, while e1 is a
(unit) vector orthogonal to e0 and to the T2 symmetry orbits. Next, e2 is a unit vector tangent
to T2 orbits (hence orthogonal to e0 and e1) and aligned with the first reference one-cycle
mentioned above. Finally, e3 is a unit vector orthogonal to the other three basis vectors. For
any given spacetime with T2 symmetry on T3, the choice of adapted moving frame depends
only on discrete data: the cycle of T2 along which e2 is aligned (with possible choices differing
by SL(2, Z) transformations), and the orientations of e3 and e1. The modular parameter τ
depends additionally on the second reference cycle.
Metric decomposition. In the so-called areal gauge, with the coordinates t, x introduced
above, any T2 symmetric spacetime metric g(1+3) on the torus T3 can be written in the form
g(1+3) = Ω2(−dt2 + λ2dx2) + |t|eP  dy + Q dz + (G + QH) dx
2 + |t|e−P (dz + H dx)2, (2.5)
where the metric coefficients P, Q, Ω, λ, G, H, with Ω, λ > 0, depend only on t ∈I and
x ∈S1 ≃[0, 1], whereas the remaining ‘parallel’ variables y, z describe T2 ≃[0, 1]2. Observe
that the vector fields ∂/∂y and ∂/∂z are Killing fields; for the existence of this metric
decomposition, we refer to Rendall [58]. In our analysis, we will sometimes find it convenient
to specialize the general results to the following important cases.
1 We may choose λ|t=t0 to be a constant. In the vacuum and under Gowdy symmetry, this choice is natural
as λ then remains constant throughout the spacetime. For general spacetimes, however, this choice depends on
the initial time slice.
24


## Page 25


• Gowdy-symmetric spacetimes, by definition, are characterized by the condition G = H = 0
throughout the spacetime. Geometrically, this is equivalent, up to an affine coordinate
change, to the vanishing of the twist variables K2, K3, which will be introduced below.
This condition implies also the vanishing1 of the parallel fluid variables J2, J3. In addition,
polarized Gowdy-symmetric spacetimes are further characterized by Q = 0.
• Vacuum T2 symmetric spacetimes are defined by setting µ = 0 throughout the spacetime,
in which case the Euler equations are automatically satisfied for an arbitrary (and
physically irrelevant) velocity field u.

Fluid variables. Throughout, the matter flow is assumed to enjoy the same T2 symmetry as

Structural properties. The proof of the following properties is postponed to Section C.1.
We emphasize that our formulation is meaningful, in particular, in vacuum regions.
Proposition 2.4 (Structure of the first-order formulation). Consider the Einstein–Euler
system under the assumption of T2 symmetry on T3 for a general equation of state satisfy-
ing (1.8). After expressing the spacetime metric g(1+3) in areal gauge (2.5), consider the metric
unknowns λ, Ω, P, Q, G, H, together with the fluid unknowns µ, u arising in the stress-energy
tensor (1.2). Assume furthermore that the solutions under consideration are sufficiently regular.
30


## Page 31


1. Hyperbolicity.
• Under the non-vacuum condition −J · J > 0, in terms of the unknowns (J, K, L)
defined in (2.8) and (λ, Ω) and subjected to the causality inequalities (2.33), the evolu-
tion system consisting of (2.29), (2.30), and (2.31) is a first-order hyperbolic system
of 12 nonlinear balance laws, which is supplemented with the 3 constraints (2.32).
(In addition, the wave equation for the lapse (2.37), below, follows from this set of
equations and, in vacuum, it is a consequence of the geometry equations alone.)
• If the sound speed p′(0) > 0 is positive, the system remains hyperbolic even in
vacuum.
• On the other hand, if the sound speed p′(0) = 0 vanishes, the system fails to be
hyperbolic in the vacuum −J · J = 0, and it is then only weakly hyperbolic.
2. Nonlinearities.
• Away from vacuum, the double characteristic family associated with the speed −λ−1 J1
J0
is linearly degenerate. Moreover, the two acoustic characteristic families associated
with the speeds ξ± are genuinely nonlinear in the sense of Lax if and only if the
pressure law satisfies the convexity condition (1.8b).
• The metric evolution equations (2.29) and (2.31) and the fluid evolution equa-
tions (2.30) contain only (at most) quadratic terms in (J, K, L), with the exception
of q-dependent terms which are sub-quadratic.
• With the exception of the lapse equations in (2.32a) and (2.31b), all quadratic source
terms involving only the variables J⊥, L are null forms —while this is not the case
for nonlinearities involving the variables J∥, K.
3. Equivalence.
• Given a solution (J, K, L) and (λ, Ω) to the equations (2.29), (2.30), and (2.31),
and provided the conditions (2.32) hold on a slice of constant area t0, one recovers
a unique solution1 to the Einstein–Euler equations: the metric coefficients P, Q are
determined by direct integration from (2.7), while G, H are immediately recovered
from (2.7).
• Next, the fluid variables µ and u are determined from (2.12a) in all non-vacuum
regions, as well as the constraints (2.32). In vacuum regions, one simply has µ = 0
and the velocity field u is ill-defined. (In addition, the wave equation (2.37), stated
below, holds for all areal times.)
4. Propagation property. Once the equations (2.32) on log Ω, K2, K3 are satisfied on an
initial hypersurface, they also hold for all future times.
The example of isothermal flows is considered in Appendix B.
1 up to irrelevant shifts of P, Q by additive constants and G, H by functions of x

2.4
Derivation of the JKL formulation
Einstein’s evolution equations. We proceed and compute suitably chosen linear combina-
tions of the 10 frame components (2.17) in terms of the geometry variables (2.8). Together
with two compatibility equations already stated in (2.29), we obtain a total of 12 equations,
which we present in the following remarkable form.
• Evolution of the essential geometry. Using our notation, after elementary calculations,
the evolution equations for L = P + i Q with P = (P0, P1) and Q = (Q0, Q1) read (2.29),
as stated previously, where the operators were defined in (2.15). The two divergence
equations are the components R22 −R33 and 2 R23 of the Einstein equations, while the
curl equations are compatibility conditions satisfied by the derivatives of P and Q and
are needed to reach a closed evolution system for the first-order unknowns.
• Evolution of the conformal length density. Notably, in our formulation, only the equations
related to the quotient geometry explicitly depend on the equation of state through the
definition (2.24) of the tensor M. The component R22 + R33 of the Einstein equations is
found to be as stated1 in (2.31a). The function λ determines the conformal geometry on
the quotient manifold N = M/T2, and will play a central role when analyzing the global
foliation. In Section 3, we will see that the equation (2.31a) stated for λ determines the
volume of spacelike slices (up to the multiplicative factor |t| Ω).
• Evolution of the lapse. The metric coefficient Ωis nothing but the lapse function in
the metric decomposition (2.5), and its time derivative is given by the Ricci component
R00 + R11 −R22 −R33 of the Einstein equations, as stated in (2.31b).
• Evolution of the twists. The components R12 and R13 provide us with the time derivatives
of the twist coefficients K2, K3, namely the differential equations (2.31c)–(2.31d).
• Additional equation for the geometry. Finally, the component R00 −R11 + R22 + R33
provides us with another geometric equation (for the lapse), but its statement is postponed
to (2.37), below.
Einstein’s constraint equations. We derive separately the remaining equations for the
geometry, which are automatically satisfied once they hold on any given hypersurface of
constant areal time.
• Constraint on the lapse. While time derivatives of λ and Ωobey the equations (2.31a)
and (2.31b), respectively, only the space derivative of Ωobeys an equation. The com-
ponent R01 of the Einstein equation relates this derivative to an energy flux term, as
stated in (2.32a).
• Constraints on the twists. The components R02 and R03 of the Einstein equations give
the space derivatives of the twists as stated in (2.32b)–(2.32c).

3
Hidden entropy structure of the Euler equations
3.1
Methodology
The notion of entropy current. In order to define weak solutions to nonlinear hyperbolic
problems [32, 39] such as the Euler system derived in Section 2, it is essential to identify a
sufficiently large class of entropy currents, and their associated balance laws, implied by the
Euler equations, and to deduce from them suitable entropy inequalities for weak solutions.
In this section, we first explain the relevance of the notion of entropy current in a geometric
setting on a general foliated spacetime, and then specialize this notion to the Euler system
in torus symmetry. The discussion of entropy inequalities for weak solutions is postponed to
Section 4.
Temporarily only, let (M, g) be a time-oriented n-dimensional Lorentzian manifold endowed
with a foliation by spacelike hypersurfaces (Σt)t∈I. (The notion of entropy current can also be
defined independently of the foliation; however, the convexity condition introduced below is
formulated relative to a chosen foliation.) Let us briefly discuss the notion of entropy current
for a system of N coupled balance laws1
divg f(u) = s(u),
(3.1)
where the unknown u is a collection of tensor fields on M, namely a section of some N-
dimensional vector bundle E →M, the source term s(u) is a section of some N-dimensional
vector bundle E′ →M, and the current f(u) is a section of TM ⊗E′ →M. Here, divg
denotes the divergence operator on (M, g), with the index contraction acting on the TM
factor of TM ⊗E′.
Both the source and the current depend on u without derivatives (and on the metric g),
and we assume that
the time component map u 7→f 0(u) is one-to-one on its image.

unit normal vector field to the foliation and XΣ is tangent to the foliation slices, and we extend
this decomposition of TM to TM ⊗E′ by a tensor product.
In local coordinates, the balance law (3.1) takes the form

(3.3)

Definition 3.7. A regular map F = (F 0, F 1)(J) is called a quasi-current for the Euler

Clearly, when the shear-induced factors vanish identically, or when the fluid velocity is
orthogonal to T2 symmetry orbits, a quasi-current reduces to an ordinary entropy current.
The notion of future convexity introduced in Definition 3.1 for currents applies likewise to
quasi-currents. Interestingly, there exist infinitely many quasi-currents, whether future-convex
or not, and this collection will be sufficient to apply compensated compactness arguments
—in contrast with the (too narrow) set of standard entropies. Moreover, the quasi-currents
F = F(J) can be conveniently parametrized by solving a second-order wave equation in phase
space; cf. [36].
Our terminology “quasi-current” is justified by the fact that the associated balance law is not
purely conservative: besides the divergence part, it contains the products F ∨
m(J) bJm,x. Entropy
quasi-currents behave like entropy currents modulo non-conservative corrections, which will be
well-defined under our regularity and integrability conditions.

3.5
The notion of dominated quasi-current
Equation of state for isentropic fluids. Weak solutions cannot satisfy all of the entropy
balance laws we exhibited in Section 3: a selection of only four balance laws shall serve as
the main set of evolution equations for the fluid variable J, which we may write as equalities.
Additionally, other future-convex entropy currents can also be used, but only as entropy
inequalities.
We treat compressible fluids whose equation of state depends on a single thermodynamic
variable, namely the proper energy-density; the physical entropy variable is thus assumed to
be a constant. As long as the solutions remain sufficiently regular, for both relativistic and
non-relativistic flows, the physical entropy (usually denoted by S) is constant; however for
weak solutions, the constancy of the physical entropy is regarded as an approximation of a flow
described by two thermodynamical variables. On the other hand, a drawback of the method
of compensated compactness for fluids, already pointed out by DiPerna in the 80’s, that it
does not apply to the Euler equation when the pressure depends upon two thermodynamical
variable. On the other hand, this method has the distinguished advantage to apply to fluid
flow with large data and thus to cover realistic hyperbolic models from continuum physics.
Our proposal in this paper. Here, we extend to relativistic flows the choice that was made
by DiPerna in his pioneering work on the compensated compactness method for non-relativistic
fluid flows and, in order for the theory proposed in the present paper to be consistent with
DiPerna’s theory in the non-relativistic limit (cf. Section D.2), it is natural to treat the stress-
energy equation associated with the vector field M0• as a mathematical entropy inequality.
To proceed, we need a convexity property, stated now. With this standpoint, the variable
N0 = −b
NJJ0 and stress-tensor components T 0m = (−1/2)J0Jm, m = 1, 2, 3, play the role
of the principal variables1. As a result, we will need the following property, whose proof is
postponed to Section D.4.
1 A different choice of entropy inequality can be made, which will be explored in Section 10, below.

4
The class of tame Einstein–Euler flows
4.1
Volume forms and integrability
The conformal length. We now introduce the notion of weak solution to the Einstein–Euler
equations. In the first part of the section, the lapse function Ω> 0 is assumed to be absolutely
continuous; cf. Definition 4.2. A weaker notion of solution, requiring only BV regularity
for Ω, is introduced later in Section 4.5. Throughout, all functions and tensor fields under
consideration are assumed to be T2-symmetric on T3. Our formulation relies in an essential
way on the hyperbolic and nonlinear structure of the field equations exhibited in Section 2,
on the entropy structure uncovered in Section 3, and, later on, on the a priori estimates
established in Sections 6 and 7.
We begin with the quotient T3/T2 ≃S1 at fixed areal time t ∈I. Fixing a coordinate x
on S1, we write the conformal length measure in the form1
dxℓ= λ dx,
λ > 0.
(4.1)
Equivalently, λ is the density of the measure dxℓwith respect to the Lebesgue measure dx.
We also introduce the conformal length function on the universal cover of S1 = R/Z,

For each fixed t ∈I, the function x 7→ℓ(t, x) is increasing, and is not periodic on S1; instead,
ℓ(t, x+1) = ℓ(t, x)+ℓ(t, 1). We often consider ℓas a function on S1 by identifying S1 ≃[0, 1) ⊂R,
as otherwise norms of ℓwould be infinite. In the sequel, we will use interchangeably the density
λ, the measure dxℓ, and the primitive ℓ, all defined on the quotient circle S1 ≃T3/T2.
Notation. The solutions under consideration are defined on an open time interval (t0, t1)
whose closure does not contain 0. We introduce the spacelike, spacetime, and timelike volume
forms by
dvol(3) := |t| Ωdxℓdy dz = |t| Ωλ dx dy dz,
dvol(1+3) := Ωdt dvol(3),
dvol(1+2) := |t| Ωdt dy dz.
(4.3)
These are, respectively, the induced volume forms on the spacelike hypersurfaces of constant
time t, on spacetime, and on the timelike hypersurfaces of constant x ∈S1. Unless otherwise
specified, all integrability properties stated below are understood with respect to these volume
forms.
We find it convenient to adopt an essentially coordinate-independent presentation. Ac-
cordingly, we assume that the spacetime is foliated by spacelike slices labelled by a time
parameter t ∈I, together with timelike hypersurfaces labelled by a space parameter x ∈S1.
The parameter x may be regarded only as a label for the one-dimensional quotient T3/T2 ≃S1,
rather than as a preferred global coordinate. Hence, in the following, we systematically use the
notation (4.3).

4.2
The notion of ΦΨ flow with finite energy
Relevant variables. It is useful to distinguish between the regularity and integrability
assumptions needed to define weak solutions to the Einstein–Euler equations in first-order
form, and the further properties that are later shown to follow from the equations.
We work on a compact interval I ⊂R not containing 0, and consider flows on M =
I × T3. In addition to the conformal length measure dxℓand the lapse Ω, the geometry is
described by the variables P = (P0, P1), Q = (Q0, Q1), K = (K2, K3), while the fluid variables
are J⊥= (J0, J1) and J∥= (J2, J3). Throughout, the causality conditions (2.33) and the
periodicity constraints (2.34) are assumed. The weak regularity assumptions are stated below
in Definition 4.1.
• Fluid variables. The fluid momentum is denoted by J = (J⊥, J∥), where J⊥and J∥are
respectively the components orthogonal and tangent to the T2-symmetry orbits.
• Essential geometric variables. The spacetime geometry is described by the first-order
variables P, Q : I × T3 →R2.
• Conformal length measure. On the quotient S1 ≃T3/T2, we consider the positive measure
dxℓ= λ dx, where x is a fixed coordinate on S1. Equivalently, one may work with the
density λ > 0 or with its primitive ℓdefined in (4.2). This determines the family of
spacelike volume forms dvol(3)(t) = |t| Ω(t) dxℓ(t) dy dz (for t ∈I).
• Lapse function. The lapse Ω: I × T3 →(0, +∞) determines the family of timelike
volume forms dvol(1+2)(·, x) = |t| Ω(·, x) dt dy dz (for x ∈S1) and the spacetime volume
form dvol(1+3).
• Twist variables. The functions Km : I × T3 →R (for m = 2, 3) measure the non-
integrability of the orthogonal complement to the symmetry orbits.
48


## Page 49


A notion of flow. Our notion of weak solution will require the following integrability and
weak regularity properties.
Definition 4.1. A ΦΨ flow with finite energy is a collection of time-dependent fields,
satisfying the causality inequalities (2.33) and the periodicity constraints (2.34), and written in
the form
Φ = (J⊥, J∥, P, Q),
Ψ = (ℓ, log Ω, K).
(4.12)
The variables Φ and Ψ are referred to as the L2-variables and the BV -variables, respectively,
and are assumed to satisfy
spacelike integrability:
Φ ∈L∞(I, L2(T3, dvol(3))),
timelike integrability:
Φ ∈L∞(S1, L2(I × T2, dvol(1+2))),
spacelike absolute continuity:
Ψ ∈L∞(I, BVac(T3)),
timelike absolute continuity:
Ψ ∈L∞(S1, BVac(I × T2)).
(4.13)
The Einstein–Euler equations discussed momentarily could be defined in the sense of
distribution under much weaker regularity conditions, specifically spacetime L2 integrability
of Φ and a uniform bound Ψ ∈L∞(I × T3). However, the conditions stated in (4.13) are
natural in the present context, and will be derived from the equations and assumptions on
initial data sets taken in the same weak regularity class; cf. Sections 6 and 7.

The definition below includes all of the Einstein–Euler equations except for the wave

To account for the entropy structure of the equations, we impose the particle number conser-

Definition 4.2. Consider the Einstein–Euler system under the assumption of T2 symmetry on
T3-spacelike slices, parametrized by an areal time function t ∈I. A ΦΨ flow with finite energy,
written as Φ = (J⊥, J∥, P, Q) and Ψ = (ℓ, log Ω, K) is called an Einstein–Euler flow if the
following conditions hold in the weak sense (4.17), (4.21), (4.24), and (4.26).
(1) Einstein’s evolution equations (2.29) and (2.31), together with Einstein’s constraint
equations (2.32).
(2) The Euler equations, consisting of the particle-number equation (3.20) and the three
momentum equations (2.30b)–(2.30d), namely

The inequality (4.29) will be referred to as the reference entropy inequality for the fluid.
As is well known, weak solutions to an initial value problem are in general not unique unless
an admissibility condition is imposed. In the present setting, the choice (4.29) is natural since,
by Proposition 3.11, the current ΩM0• is future-convex for the system (4.28). Hence, the notion
introduced in Definition 4.2 is consistent with the general entropy framework in Section 3.1.
Remark 4.3. A conceptually important feature of the present theory is that it also admits an
alternative formulation, obtained by exchanging which one of the particle number current −N
and mass energy M0• obeys an equality or an inequality. Indeed, the compactness framework
developed in the present paper is not based on a rigid choice of entropy relation, but on a
more intrinsic structure of the Einstein–Euler system. This alternative viewpoint is discussed
in Section 10.1.
52


## Page 53


The class of tame flows. For elementary wave patterns, such as solutions of the Riemann
problem, the reference entropy inequality (4.29) is sufficient to select the admissible weak
solution. For general weak solutions, however, no uniqueness theory is currently available at
the level of regularity considered here. Furthermore, our subsequent compactness arguments
require a sufficiently large family of entropy quasi-currents. These entropies are not imposed
by the physics; rather, they are introduced in order to rule out possibly oscillatory sequences of
solutions and to justify the passage to the limit in the nonlinear fluid terms, as well as in the
coupling between matter and geometry.
In view of the quasi-currents presented in Section 3, we therefore strengthen the preceding
notion of weak solution. The resulting formulation is slightly delicate, since the quasi-balance
laws involve shear-induced factors containing derivatives of the parallel momentum per particle.
At this stage, it is useful to introduce the parallel momentum per particle (cf. Definition 3.4)

2.
Propagation of the constraints.
Concerning the property of propagation of the con-
straints (2.32), we observe that our argument in the proof of Proposition 2.4 (cf. Section C.1)
remains valid for weak solutions, provided all the identities are understood in the sense of
distributions. Indeed, commuting derivatives is valid for distributions and, in our argument
in Section C.1, the identity Lt = 0 for a distribution L implies that L is independent of t.
Importantly, the propagation of constraints on (log Ω, K2, K3) only requires the three spatial
Euler equations and not the energy balance law that only holds as an inequality.
4.5
The notion of weak solution with corrector
Stress-energy correctors. When considering limits of sequences of solutions (in Section 5,
below), we are going to obtain two results depending on whether the initial data sets are
assumed to be well-prepared or not. When initial data sets are not well-prepared, the notion
of solution described so far is insufficient to describe the limit. It is necessary to introduce a
generalized notion of weak solution, in which some of the Einstein–Euler equations are imposed
only modulo suitable correctors, as we now explain. These new terms will be measures arising
from oscillations of the geometry.
The key modification is that we no longer require the lapse Ωto be absolutely continuous.
Instead, the analysis of weakly convergent sequences suggests that the natural regularity is to
require that Ωhave bounded variation in space along each spacelike slice and in time along
each timelike slice.
To describe the correctors, we introduce the space L∞(I, M(T3)), consisting of all weakly-
star measurable maps t ∈I 7→m(t), where m(t) is a bounded Radon measure on T3. Here
M(T3) denotes the dual of C(T3), endowed with the sup norm. Thus, the measure m(t)
need not be absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure and may therefore
contain singular parts, including Dirac masses. Weak-star measurability means that, for every
φ ∈C(T3), the map t 7→⟨m(t), φ⟩is Lebesgue measurable on I. We also use the space
L∞(I, BV (T3)) of functions that have bounded variation along the spacelike slices.
Let us begin with a formal motivation of how correctors arise as a result of weak L2
convergence of (a sequence of) geometric variables (Pϵ, Qϵ) to some limit (P♯, Q♯). Squares such

Definition 4.8. An Einstein–Euler flow with corrector is a ΦΨΠ flow with finite energy

the product with Radon measures.

Boundary terms at the initial time. Thanks to the notation above, initial data can be in-
corporated directly into the weak formulation by allowing test functions that are compactly
supported in the half-closed interval [t0, t1). In this way, Stokes’ formula produces a boundary
contribution at the initial time t0, and we adopt the resulting identities as our definition of
weak solutions with prescribed initial data. If desired, one could of course include analogous
boundary contributions at a final time t1, but this will not be needed here.
